AI 資金壓力拖累科技股，惟產業需求仍具韌性
2026 年 10 月 23 日
一、 市場動態
1. 費半再度重挫，科技股成為美股主要賣壓來源：美國股市主要指數 10 月 22 日跌多漲
少，道瓊工業指數上漲 0.02%，收 52,867.98 點；S&P 500 指數下跌 1.21%，收 7,663.43
點；Nasdaq 綜合指數下跌 2.60%，收 26,008.86 點，創 6 月 5 日以來最大單日跌幅
。費城半導體指數重挫 6.72%，收 10,731.98 點，跌幅明顯大於主要股價指數，顯示
AI 及半導體族群仍是本波市場修正的主要壓力來源。

2. AI 相關企業全面承壓，資金流向非科技股及公債：10 月 22 日 Meta 下跌約 7%、Lam
Research 跌幅逾一成，Nvidia 下跌 4.4%，其他 AI 相關股票亦明顯走低，顯示市場
對 AI 資本支出及投資報酬的疑慮已由大型科技平台延伸至半導體與基礎建設供應商
。不過，道瓊上漲 0.02%，羅素 2000 指數亦小幅收紅，反映資金並未全面撤出股市
；美國 10 年期公債殖利率則因避險需求跌破 5%，顯示資金由高評價科技股轉向其他
產業及避險資產。
3. 中東地緣政治風險升溫，油價攀至 5 月以來高點：中東能源運輸及供給風險 10 月 22
日再度升高，布蘭特原油價格上漲約 3.76%，升至每桶 113 美元附近，創 5 月以來高
點。油價攀升除使市場重新關注能源成本對通膨與 Fed 政策路徑的影響，也可能增加
AI 資料中心的電力及營運成本。中東情勢雖非本次半導體股重挫的唯一因素，但在市
場已擔憂 AI 投資效益的情況下，能源價格上揚進一步壓抑投資人風險偏好。
二、 下跌原因分析：

1. 殖利率回落難抵科技股下跌，顯示修正因素出現轉變：10 月 14 日費半重挫主要受到
美國 9 月 CPI 高於預期、升息疑慮及美債殖利率盤中急升影響，惟當日 10 年期殖利
率收盤反而小幅下降；10 月 22 日情況則更加明顯，美國 10 年期公債殖利率因避險
需求盤中跌破 5%，費半仍大跌 6.72%。這顯示市場關注焦點已由單純的利率與估值
壓力，逐步轉向 AI 資本支出的資金來源、融資能力及投資回收期。因此，即使後續殖
利率回落，若企業仍無法證明 AI 投資能轉化為足夠營收與現金流，科技股評價仍可能
持續受到壓抑。
2. Meta 自由現金流轉負，AI 投資成本由預期轉為實際財務壓力：Meta 第三季營收 646.0
億美元、年增 26%，顯示核心業務仍維持強勁成長；但資本支出達 349.1 億美元，超
過同期營業現金流 327.9 億美元，使自由現金流由去年同期正 106.3 億美元轉為負
21.2 億美元。公司亦將全年資本支出預估調整至 1,350～1,450 億美元，並宣布設立

無表決權 Class C 股票，增加未來資本運用彈性。這反映 AI 投資規模已開始超越部分

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
大型科技企業當期現金創造能力，但 Meta 仍持有 824.5 億美元現金、約當現金及有
價證券，不能將單季自由現金流轉負直接解讀為財務危機。
3. 資本稀缺逐漸成為 AI 擴張瓶頸，新興雲端業者面臨更大壓力：AI 發展需要持續投入
GPU、伺服器、資料中心及電力基礎建設，龐大資金需求使融資能力逐漸成為決定企
業成長速度的重要因素。相較具備穩定營業現金流與龐大資產規模的大型科技平台，
新興 AI 雲端服務商如 CoreWeave 及 Nebius 高度依賴外部融資，因而更容易受到借
貸成本、信用利差及投資人風險偏好變化影響。我們認為，這次修正並不代表 AI 運算

需求突然消失，而是市場開始重新評估不同企業能否負擔下一階段的擴張成本。
4. 半導體設備財報強勁卻遭賣壓，市場提高獲利兌現標準：Lam Research 於 10 月 21
日公布截至 2026 年 9 月季度（FY2027 Q1）營收 82.19 億美元，年增 54.4%、季增
22%，非 GAAP 營業利益率達 40.2%，下一季營收指引為 86 億美元、上下浮動 4 億
美元。然而，即使營運仍快速成長，10 月 22 日股價仍大幅下跌。我們研判，市場已
由過去重視 AI 訂單與營收成長，轉向同時檢視未來成長能否超越原先偏高預期，以及
AI 投資是否具備足夠的財務報酬。當股價已反映高度成長期待，即使財報表現良好，
也可能因評價調整與籌碼降溫而承壓。
5. 油價再度上漲，Fed 政策風險仍可能限制科技股評價：儘管 10 月 22 日美債殖利率因
避險需求下滑，但能源價格上漲仍可能延緩通膨降溫，使 Fed 後續政策路徑維持不確
定性。若油價持續高檔，不僅可能推升企業運輸、電力及營運成本，也將增加通膨預

期，進而限制長天期美債殖利率的下行空間。因此，科技股短線同時面臨 AI 投資報酬
受到重新檢視，以及能源價格與利率政策不確定的雙重壓力。後續 10 月 28 日 FOMC
會後聲明及主席記者會，將是評估利率環境的重要依據。
三、 市場看法：
1. 股市下跌但多數個股上漲，顯示賣壓集中於大型科技股：10 月 22 日 S&P 500 指數下
跌 1.21%，但根據美聯社（AP）報導，約三分之二成分股逆勢上漲，追蹤 S&P 500
等權重指數的 ETF（RSP）更上漲 0.73%，道瓊工業指數亦小幅收紅，呈現市值加權
指數與市場廣度明顯背離的現象。這反映本波修正主要集中於權重較高的 AI 及大型科
技股，而非整體美股同步走弱。相較上週以防禦性類股輪動為主的情況，本週更值得
注意的是市場對高資本支出及高評價科技企業的重新定價，資金仍持續流向其他產業
，而非全面性風險撤離。只要賣壓未進一步擴散，現階段仍較接近科技股評價調整與
類股輪動，而非整體美股多頭格局反轉。
2. 台積電財測再度上修，顯示 AI 需求尚未出現結構性反轉：台積電 10 月 15 日法說會

將 2026 年美元營收成長預估由原先略高於 40%上修至約 44%，第三季營收達新台幣
1.49 兆元、年增 50.9%，稅後純益年增 68.7%，每股盈餘 29.43 元、同樣年增 68.7%
；第四季美元營收預估為 523～543 億美元，以中間值計算季增約 14%。此外，Lam
Research 截至 9 月季度營收年增 54.4%，亦顯示半導體設備需求仍強勁。前述這些

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
數據與近期科技股重挫形成明顯對比，反映目前市場主要擔憂 AI 投資回收及股票評價
，而非半導體終端需求同步惡化。只要大型雲端服務商未全面縮減 AI 資本支出，先進
製程與設備需求中期仍具支撐。
3. AI 投資進入報酬驗證階段，營收成長能否追上資本投入成為關鍵：我們認為，AI 產業
目前正由過去的資本支出擴張階段，逐步轉向投資報酬率與自由現金流的驗證階段。
以 Meta 為例，第三季營收仍成長 26%，但資本支出已超過營業現金流，顯示即使
AI 技術有助於改善廣告推薦及營收變現，龐大基礎建設投入仍可能使短期自由現金流

承壓。不過，資料中心及運算設備通常具有多年使用年限，不能僅以單季現金流評估
整體投資成敗。真正關鍵在於未來 AI 服務收入、營業利益及現金流成長，能否逐步抵
銷前期資本投入。具備明確商業化模式、較高資本效率及穩定現金流的企業，後續表
現可望優於高度依賴融資與成長預期的業者。
4. AI 融資風險仍集中於部分企業，尚不足以認定信用危機全面擴散：Meta Q3 財報顯示
，該公司持有 824.5 億美元現金、約當現金及有價證券，顯示其具備一定財務緩衝能
力；相較之下，新興 AI 雲端業者因資料中心建置成本龐大、現金流尚未成熟，對信用
市場與外部融資條件的敏感度相對較高。雖然 AI 資本支出引發投資人重新評估企業信
用風險，但我們認為目前仍缺乏足夠證據認定風險已擴散至整體投資級債市場或銀行
體系。然而，後續仍宜密切觀察 AI 相關企業 CDS 與公司債利差變化、再融資能力及
主要金融機構授信條件對於科技類股及整體股債市場的後續影響。
5. 股價修正不代表企業獲利同步下修，財報表現仍有機會支撐科技股：儘管近期半導體

及 AI 相關股價回落，但台積電與 Lam Research 最新財報顯示，部分供應鏈企業營收
及獲利仍維持高度成長，反映股票價格與企業基本面短期可能出現明顯落差。當市場
因融資及資本支出疑慮而降低評價倍數時，即使企業獲利持續增加，股價也可能暫時
承壓。然而，若後續大型科技公司公布的雲端營收、AI 訂單與獲利展望持續優於預期
，企業 EPS 上修仍有機會抵銷部分本益比壓縮壓力。因此，目前尚不宜將科技股價格
修正直接等同於 AI 產業景氣反轉，企業獲利能否持續兌現，仍是中期市場方向的重要
支撐。

策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
6. Fed 與大型科技股財報進入驗證期，短線震盪可能延續：Fed 將於 10 月 27～28 日舉
行利率決策會議，市場除關注是否調整政策利率外，更應重視會後聲明及主席記者會
對通膨、油價與後續利率路徑的態度。另一方面，大型雲端服務商後續公布的財報，
將提供 AI 資本支出、雲端營收及投資效率的最新證據。短線科技股仍可能受到市場評
價偏高、融資風險及地緣政治因素影響而維持震盪；不過，只要企業獲利成長與 AI
需求未出現結構性反轉，中期科技產業基本面仍具支撐，現階段維持短線審慎、中期
偏正向的看法。
四、市場展望：

1. 科技股短線宜審慎觀察，中期仍看好具基本面支撐的 AI 產業：近期科技股受到 AI 資本支
出、融資能力與評價調整等因素影響，短線波動可能延續，尤其高度依賴外部資金及獲利
尚未成熟的 AI 基礎建設業者，仍面臨較大不確定性。然而，台積電及 Lam Research 最
新財報顯示，先進製程與半導體設備需求尚未出現全面轉弱。我們認為，科技股中期展望
仍偏正向，但市場將更重視企業營收成長、訂單能見度、自由現金流及資本效率。相較缺
乏穩定獲利的 AI 概念企業，具技術優勢、財務結構穩健及獲利成長能見度的科技企業，
風險報酬相對有利。
2. AI 產業由全面成長轉向基本面分化，現金流將成為選股關鍵：隨著大型科技企業持續擴
大 AI 基礎建設投資，市場對未來投資報酬率的要求將同步提高。就目前已公布的科技股
財報結果來看，AI 產業長期成長趨勢尚未改變，但後續股價表現將更加分化，具備穩定
現金流、明確訂單及商業化能力的企業，相對有利；反之，對於高度依賴外部融資、自由
現金流持續為負且缺乏明確獲利時程的企業，仍宜維持審慎。

3. 美債避險功能重現，中天期債券風險報酬相對均衡：半導體類股 10/22 重挫，美國公債
價格上揚、10 年期殖利率盤中跌破 5%，顯示股市風險偏好下降時，美債仍可提供一定
避險效果。雖然 Fed 政策路徑及能源價格仍存在不確定性，但預期相較受政策利率影響
較大的短天期公債，以及受財政赤字、國債供給與期限溢價影響的超長天期公債，中天期
美國公債的風險報酬仍相對較為均衡。投資級債因企業信用基本面整體仍具韌性，中期維
持偏正向看法；高收益債則須留意 AI 相關發行人信用風險上升及利差擴大的影響。

1

國際經濟金融週報內容摘要 
美 
國 
美國經濟維持穩健擴張，通膨壓力略升溫，Fed 維持利率不變，期中選舉進入
最後衝刺階段 
經濟 
⚫ 美國第三季實質 GDP 季增年率(初值)由上季的 2.2%升至 3.4%，成長主
要受民間消費、企業投資及庫存回補帶動。其中個人消費貢獻 2.52 個百
分點、私人投資貢獻 3.27 個百分點，惟淨出口拖累 3.32 個百分點，整
體經濟仍維持穩健擴張。 
⚫ 9 月美國新屋銷售年化 67.6 萬戶，月減 0.6%、年減 5.3%，主要是中西
部及南部地區銷售下滑拖累整體表現。房價雖較上月回升，但仍低於去
年同期；供給月數 8.4 個月，顯示市場仍呈供給充裕、需求偏弱格局。 
⚫ 美國 9 月個人消費支出(PCE)物價指數年增率由 3.4%升至 3.6%，核心
PCE 物價指數年增率亦由 3.0%升至 3.1%，通膨再度升溫。儘管實質所
得受物價上漲影響而下滑，消費支出仍持續成長，反映家庭需求及內需
動能仍具韌性。 
⚫ 10 月 FOMC 會議以 10 比 2 決議將聯邦基金利率目標區間維持於 3.75%
至 4.0%。決策聲明指出，美國經濟活動表現強勁，就業市場變化不大，
通膨持續高於 2%目標，將依後續數據評估適當政策立場，以確保通膨
回歸目標。兩名委員主張升息 1 碼，顯示內部對通膨風險仍存分歧。主
席 Kevin Warsh 強調將防止能源價格上漲擴散至整體物價，並宣布 2027
年起部分 FOMC 會議改為策略討論會議，以強化長期政策研議。 
重要 
事件 
⚫ 10 月 26 日路透社民調顯示，川普施政支持率 32%，略高於前週；國會
選舉政黨支持度則由民主黨 47%領先共和黨 36%。截至 10 月 31 日，
提前及郵寄投票已逾 5,100 萬人。整體而言，期中選舉結果將影響未來
國會版圖、施政推動及貿易政策方向，備受市場關注。 
短評 
⚫ 美國第三季 GDP 成長優於前季，主要受民間消費、企業投資及庫存回
補帶動，顯示內需與企業活動仍具韌性；惟新屋銷售持續疲弱、房市需
求未明顯改善，反映高利率環境仍抑制部分經濟活動。另一方面，PCE
通膨再度升溫，核心通膨降幅有限，顯示物價壓力仍存。在經濟穩健且
通膨高於目標情況下，Fed 維持利率不變並釋出偏鷹訊號，後續貨幣政
策料將維持審慎，持續關注通膨與就業數據變化。

一、 美國—經濟維持穩健擴張，通膨壓力略升溫，Fed 維持利率不變，期中選
舉進入最後衝刺階段 
2026 年第三季美國實質國內生產毛額(GDP)季增年率初值為 3.4%，高於第二
季之 2.2%，整體景氣持續穩健擴張。就成長貢獻而言，個人消費支出仍為經濟成
長主要支柱，貢獻 2.52 個百分點，其中服務消費及商品消費分別貢獻 1.89 個百
分點及 0.63 個百分點，反映民間需求維持穩健；私人投資則貢獻 3.27 個百分點，
為本季成長最大亮點，其中固定投資貢獻 1.66 個百分點，而企業庫存回補亦額外
貢獻 1.61 個百分點。對外貿易方面，淨出口拖累經濟成長 3.32 個百分點，其中
出口減少造成 0.68 個百分點負面影響；進口增加則拖累 2.64 個百分點。政府消
費支出與投資則受聯邦政府非國防支出增加帶動，貢獻 0.90 個百分點。此外，第
三季個人消費支出(PCE)物價指數及核心 PCE 物價指數分別年增 2.1%及 2.5%，
均低於第二季水準。整體而言，美國第三季經濟成長主要受民間消費、企業投資
及庫存回補支撐，雖貿易逆差擴大對 GDP 形成明顯拖累，但內需表現仍具韌性，
顯示美國經濟基本面維持穩健。 
2026 年 9 月美國新屋銷售經季節性調整後年化戶數為 67.6 萬戶，較上月減
少 0.6%，並較去年同期下降 5.3%，顯示在高利率環境下住宅需求仍較去年疲弱。
地區別表現分歧，其中東北部及西部分別較上月增加 28.0%及 21.4%，惟中西部
及南部分別減少 7.3%及 6.6%，尤其占全美銷售比重最大的南部地區回落，成為
拖累整體銷售的主因。價格方面，9 月新屋銷售中位價為 40.04 萬美元，較上月
上升 3.9%，但較去年同期下跌 3.9%，儘管房價高於上月，惟仍面臨修正壓力。
供給方面，9 月底待售新屋數量為 47.6 萬戶，較上月減少 1.0%，較去年同期減少
2.3%；按目前銷售速度計算，供給月數為 8.4 個月，低於上月的 8.5 個月，但高
於去年同期的 8.2 個月。整體而言，9 月美國新屋銷售較前月小幅下滑且年減幅
度擴大，反映購屋需求仍未明顯改善；雖然部分地區銷售回升且庫存略有下降，
但供給月數仍維持相對偏高水準，顯示新屋市場仍處於供給較充裕、需求偏弱的
格局，後續仍須觀察利率走勢及就業市場變化對住宅需求的影響。 
2026 年 9 月美國個人消費支出(PCE)物價指數月增 0.5%，高於 8 月之 0.3%，

年增率則由 3.4%升至 3.6%；扣除食品及能源後之核心 PCE 月增率維持於 0.3%，
年增率由 3.0%微升至 3.1%，能源價格回升及服務價格上漲持續對整體物價形成
支撐，通膨再度升溫。另一方面，個人所得月增 0.2%，可支配所得(DPI)亦月增
0.2%，個人消費支出則月增 0.5%，增幅高於所得成長，顯示家庭消費需求仍具韌
性；惟受物價上漲影響，實質可支配所得月減 0.3%，實質個人消費支出則持平，
反映名目支出增加部分已被物價上升所抵銷。整體而言，美國 9 月消費活動維持
穩健，內需仍為經濟成長提供支撐，但通膨壓力較前月略有升溫，物價回落至聯
準會 2%目標之過程仍具挑戰，後續仍須持續關注能源價格變化、核心通膨走勢
及消費動能對聯準會貨幣政策方向之影響。 
聯準會(Fed)於 2026 年 10 月 FOMC 會議以 10 比 2 表決結果，決定將聯邦基
金利率目標區間維持於 3.75%至 4.00%不變，符合市場預期；謹將本次會議聲明
及主席 Kevin Warsh 記者會重點摘錄如下：(一)聯準會認為美國經濟活動持續以
強勁步調擴張，較前次聲明所稱之「穩健擴張」進一步上修對經濟表現之評價；
儘管地緣政治情勢使經濟前景不確定性仍偏高，但國內支出維持韌性，生產力成
長與資本投資表現強勁，就業增幅大致與勞動力成長同步，失業率變動不大，顯
示整體經濟基本面仍具相當韌性；(二)通膨仍高於聯準會 2%目標，委員會表示需
累積更多經濟與物價資訊，以評估適當政策立場，確保通膨適時回歸 2%目標，
並重申將致力維持物價穩定；(三)本次決議出現兩張反對票，聯準會理事 Lisa Cook
及克里夫蘭聯邦準備銀行總裁 Beth Hammack 主張升息 1 碼，反映部分委員對通
膨風險仍抱持較為謹慎態度；(四)主席 Kevin Warsh 於記者會表示，近期聯邦公
開市場委員會討論焦點多集中於物價穩定，特別是在近期能源及相關價格再度上
揚情況下，聯準會有責任避免個別產業價格上漲擴散至整體經濟，並確保通膨預
期維持穩定；(五)Warsh 並宣布調整 FOMC 會議安排，2027 年仍將維持每年 8 次
會議，但其中 1 次將改為專門討論貨幣政策策略之會議，不進行利率表決或發布
政策聲明，並預期 2028 年將有 2 次會議採取此模式，以騰出更多時間進行長期
政策與重大議題研議。整體而言，本次會議雖維持利率不變，但聯準會仍認為通
膨問題尚未完全解決，後續政策方向仍將取決於通膨、勞動市場及整體經濟數據
表現。 
根據 10 月 26 日路透社最新民調，總統川普施政支持率為 32%，較前一週之
31%略為回升；在國會選舉政黨支持度方面，47%的登記選民表示傾向支持民主

二、歐元區—歐洲央行如期暫停升息，12 月升息機率較高 
 歐元區第三季 GDP 季增率為 0.4%，但有部份是受到愛爾蘭經濟成長率大幅
波動的扭曲，若剔除愛爾蘭，則歐元區第三季 GDP 季增率僅約 0.2%。該季受到
物價壓力上升、地緣政治緊張局勢等因素影響，對消費及投資需求構成負面因素，
但世足賽以及德國財政支出擴張的支持下，仍使歐元區經濟保持正向表現。就國
別而言，德、法、義、西 GDP 季增率分別為 1.2%、-0.1%、0.8%、2.7%；其中德
國經濟活動受到財政擴張的提振；法國的消費支出雖強勁，但受到投資減緩和進
口增加拖累；義大利製造業產出萎縮拖累成長表現；西班牙在住宅營建熱潮的支
持下，經濟活動持續優於其他國家，但歐盟 NGEU 計畫到期以及政府財政整頓力
道，西班牙後續經濟動能可能會逐漸放緩。 
 歐洲央行在 10 月 29 日宣布維持政策利率不變，主要是近期歐元區各國殖利
率普遍上升，對經濟造成限制性因素，且不確定性可能會隨著時間的推移而累積，
從而拖累經濟成長。不過，歐洲央行仍認為近期高漲的能源價格對通膨的壓力相
當大，使央行考慮進一步採取貨幣政策措施因應。因此，除非經濟成長受到超出
預期的衝擊、能源價格下跌或金融環境急劇收緊，一般預期歐洲央行在 12 月的
政策會議將會宣布升息一碼。 
三、日本—日本央行暫停升息，通膨壓力續存，升息預期升溫 
 9 月工業生產月增 2.2%，扭轉 8 月受天災造成生產中斷而下滑的情勢，從細
項來看，汽車、電子產品及生產機械產量均呈回升，其中生產機械已連續第四個
月成長，反映 AI 相關資本支出需求持續暢旺；庫存月增 2.5%，石化產品庫存亦
逐步回補。展望未來，製造商預估 10 月及 11 月產量將分別月增 5.1%及 0.8%，

顯示在 AI 及資本支出需求、汽車復產與庫存回補支撐下，短期工業生產動能可
望維持強勁。 
 9 月零售月減 0.7%，主要受雨季抑制家庭外出消費，以及先前受新規上路前
換機需求及汽車稅減免政策帶動的耐久財消費動能逐漸減弱，致整體消費動能偏
弱；惟服裝及一般商品銷售受假期延長與節慶需求支撐而有所回升。另 10 月消
費者信心指數下降 0.4 點至 35.0，連續第二個月下滑，反映消費者對物價上漲及
實質購買力下降的憂慮加深，預期未來一年通膨率超過 2%的消費者比重亦升至
79.8%。展望未來，隨天候等暫時性不利因素逐漸消退，加上所得成長仍具韌性，
第四季消費動能可望回升；惟通膨持續升溫，加上汽油補貼逐步退場，恐進一步
侵蝕購買力，為消費復甦的下行風險。 
 10 月東京核心 CPI 年增 2.6%，石油相關成本上漲已由核心商品逐步擴散至服
務價格，食品及生鮮食品價格亦偏強。雖目前能源、汽油補貼等政府措施仍抑制
通膨，但隨 12 月相關補貼逐步退場，成本壓力將進一步反映於商品及服務價格，
預期至 2026 年底潛在通膨仍可能高於央行的 2%目標，通膨黏性值得持續關注。 
 日本央行於 10 月 29、30 日召開貨幣政策會議，維持政策利率於 1.25%不變，
主要係仍需觀察先前升息的累積效果，但整體政策訊號仍偏鷹派。本次決議以 8：
1 通過，唯一反對的政策委員高田創主張再升息 1 碼至 1.5%，反映央行內部對通
膨上行風險仍具警戒。日本央行指出通膨已大致達到與 2%通膨目標相符的水準，
同時將 2027 財年的核心 CPI(不含生鮮食品)預測由 2.4%上調至 2.6%，顯示目前
通膨情勢仍支持貨幣政策進一步正常化。央行總裁表示，目前金融環境仍屬寬鬆，
未來若經濟及物價走勢符合預期，仍將適時調升政策利率，市場因而持續關注後
續會議再度升息的可能性。此外，日本央行亦決定自 2027 年 1 月起，將 ETF 出
售規模由約 3,300 億日圓提高至 8,600 億日圓，加速資產負債表正常化進程。 
四、中國—工業部門成本轉嫁能力有限，使利潤率持續萎縮 
1-9 月工業部門營業收入、利潤年增率分別為 15.5、13.1%，其中營業收入年
增率較上期(1-8 月)大增 8.9 個百分點，但利潤年增率下降 4.5 個百分點。受惠於
金屬、塑化原物料上漲，加上 AI 需求擴張，以及政府推動高科技產業發展等因
素，營收成長貢獻度較高者包含有色金屬加工(營收、利潤年增 17.5%、79.7%)、
計算機、通信和其他電子設備製造業(營收、利潤年增 21%、103.8%)、化學原料

及製品(營收、利潤年增 9.4%、49.2%)。此外，電氣機械及器材業、汽車業營收年
增率雖分別為 11.3%、2.6%，但獲利分別年減 5.9%、22.8%。非金屬礦物製品業
營收、獲利下滑 9.7、46.9%，黑色金屬加工業營收、獲利分別年減 1.5%、73.3%，
反映產業持續進行減產所致。整體而言，工業部門營收受到物價上漲帶動，但成
本轉嫁能力有限，反而降低利潤表現；部分產業受惠於短期原物料價格波動，以
及受到政策推動的高科技產業表現較佳，惟部分產業因陷入產能調整週期，或政
府減少支持等因素而表現較弱，產業分化明顯。 
五、印度及東南亞—泰、菲進出口創歷史新高，泰國央行按兵不動以支撐經濟 
(1) 新加坡：9 月消費者物價指數(CPI)年增率小幅回落至 2.2%、核心通膨率自 8
月的 2.2%降至 2%，反映交通運輸（7.4%）、服裝鞋帽（2.1%）及家庭耐用品
和服務（0.2%）價格漲幅顯著放緩，此外，由於行業競爭壓力，資訊通信技術
服務價格的進一步回落也抑制整體服務價格的上漲。隨著 11 月電力和天然氣
價格下調生效，預計 2026 年第四季核心通膨將進一步放緩。 
(2) 泰國：9 月出口額達 384.4 億美元、年增 24.0%，創歷史新高，主要得益於工
業產品出口成長 25.4%，其中，以電腦及相關設備（59.4%）、電話及零件
（117.5%）及電路板（45.9%）成長最為顯著，對美國出口額年增 50.1%，對
中國和日本的出口額分別成長 18.4%和 19.0%。9 月進口額 443 億美元、年增
50.4%，創歷史新高，因出口導向製造業生產需求及全球能源價格的上漲，主
要類別進口額均有所成長，其中，資本貨物(電腦、機械零件、電路板等)進口
大增 92.3%，燃料、原材料和中間產品進口增幅均逾 4 成、運輸設備在飛機與
航空設備進口大增 12 倍下成長 24%。9 月貿易逆差大幅擴大至 59 億美元，累
計 1 至 9 月，貿易逆差達 437 億美元，出口額成長 19.6%、進口額成長 37.8%。
泰國央行 10 月會議維持基準利率於 1%不變，維持寬鬆的貨幣政策立場，以
支持經濟復甦的不平衡。經濟預估方面，強於預期的出口以及與科技和人工智
慧週期相關的投資，預估 2026 年和 2027 年經濟成長率將分別成長 2.4%和
2.1%，然而，高額的家庭債務、脆弱的勞動市場和疲軟的購買力繼續抑制私人
消費，使經濟成長仍低於潛在水平。通膨方面，受能源價格上漲和聖嬰現象的
影響，預計通膨率在 2027 年初將出現短暫上升，之後隨著國內需求的疲軟而
回落，2026 年和 2027 年整體通膨率平均分別為 2.1%和 1.3%。

六、台灣—AI 動能續強，經濟成長動能穩健 
9 月景氣對策信號續呈紅燈，綜合判斷分數為 41 分，較上月增加 1 分，燈號
續呈紅燈，其中工業及服務業加班工時，受惠 AI 相關產業生產動能維持熱絡，
由綠燈轉呈黃紅燈，其餘八項燈號均維持不變。另領先與同時指標皆呈上升，顯
示國內經濟動能維持穩健。 
主計總處 10 月 30 日公布台灣第三季經濟成長率為 14.36%，主要受 AI 需求
強勁帶動出口及企業投資大幅成長，民間消費亦維持熱絡，顯示外需動能正逐步
向內需擴散，整體經濟呈現內外皆熱。展望未來，AI 需求、半導體相關投資及企
業資本支出持續擴張，加上年底消費旺季，可望支撐短期景氣表現；惟今年經濟
高速成長墊高比較基期，明年增速料將放緩，後續仍須關注 AI 需求延續性、成
長動能向傳統產業擴散情形及金融市場波動等不確定因素。

Quanta Computer
Valuation
Our target price of NT$450 is based on a 14x PE multiple on 2027E earnings. The target multiple is in line with historical trading
average since 2021, mainly to reflect strong earnings growth trajectory brought about by servers/AI servers and EV
components. We use a P/E-based methodology as we believe the market will be primarily focused on Quanta's earnings
performance given the dynamics of the NB/server industry.

Risks
Downside risks that could prevent the stock from reaching our target price include: 1) slower-than-expected server/AI server
demand growth; 2) execution risks relating to margin improvement such as production yield rate following capacity relocation
or supply-chain management; and 3) market share loss of key customers.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and

Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:
Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in

reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is
moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds

our ratings distribution table for our Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside rating system. However, we reiterate that we do
not consider No View to be a recommendation. For all Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside calls, risk exists that the
catalyst(s) and associated share-price movement will not materialize as expected.

台新證券投資顧問股份有限公司 台北市仁愛路四段 118 號 16 樓(02)5589-9558  (113)金管投顧新字第 021 號 
公司拜訪快報

雙鴻(3324)：3Q26 財報不如預期，營運逐季向上 
建議：維持買進

一、雙鴻為散熱解決方案供應商  雙鴻成立於 1998 年 8 月，為國內前二大 NB 散熱模組廠，過去主要生產散熱
器、散熱片、散熱模組、散熱導管、風扇等，現在已轉型為提供客戶完整散熱解
決方案供應商。主要應用於筆記型電腦、桌上型電腦、智慧型手機及伺服器等領
域。3Q26 產品應用領域 Server 81%、PC 14%、VGA/Gaming 4%、及 Others(手
機及車用) 1%。另外 3Q26 水冷業務比重 62%(1H26 約為 55~60%)。

二、3Q26 毛利率 25.65%，低於
預期，3Q26 EPS 11.59 元 
 雙鴻 3Q26 EPS 11.59 元，低於台新預估的 14.13 元，獲利達成率 82%。本業
來看，3Q26 營收 97.87 億元，+12.5%QOQ，+64.3%YOY，低於市場預期之
+15~20%QOQ，七月營收有遞延認列，實際拉貨力道未如預期顯著，3Q26 毛
利率 25.65%，較 2Q26 再衰退 2.77 個百分點，亦低於台新預估的 30%，主要
是報廢品打消，預期 4Q26 毛利率回升，3Q26 EPS 11.59 元。雙鴻 10 月營收
33.72 億元，+17%MOM，+45%YOY，獲利達成率 31%，符合預期，預期 11 月
營收及 12 月營收將優於 10 月，預估 4Q26 營收 110.37 億元，+12.8%QOQ，
+45.6%YOY，預估 4Q26 毛利率 28%，預估 4Q26 EPS 14.79 元。

三、營運逐季成長，氣冷及水冷
兩塊業務均樂觀 
 2025 年雙鴻水冷營收比重 35%，1Q26 提升至 55%，3Q26 提升至 62%，預期
2027 年將提升至 80%，帶動公司業績大幅增長。氣冷部分，2024 年月營收 2-
2.5 億元，2025 年 4～4.5 億元，2026 年平均 6～7 億元，氣冷水冷均樂觀，以
AWS TR3 來看，氣冷及水冷比重至少是 70~80%對比 20~30%，TR4 將為 100%
全水冷，水冷大趨勢不變，VR 9 月起量，VR 營收逐季增，TR3 9 月起量 Compute 
tray 及 Switch tray 及 Rack manifolds 都有相關產品出貨，預計 12 月單月 ASIC
營收比重有機會來到 15~25%，ASIC 水冷產值高於GPU 客戶，其中 Mata>AWS，
2027 年 ASIC 增長可期，公司預期 2027 年 ASIC 營收將超過 GPU。2026 年可
視為 CDU 元年，2027 年營收跳增可期。台新預估 4Q26 營收 110.37 億元，
BVPS 11/18 收盤價 2027 PER 建議

四、維持買進理由  3Q26 毛利率仍不如預期，故下修未來各季毛利率，氣冷及水冷兩塊業務樂觀，
水冷擴張趨勢明確，故維持買進，預估 2026 年 EPS 49.26 元(原估 55.74 元)，
預估 2027 年 EPS 76.62 元(原估 90.36 元)，目標價 1200 元。

### Page 1

HD 현대중공업 (329180/KS) 
실적&임단협 우려 해소, 발전엔진 수요 폭발적 
2026-10-29

조선/방산. 한승한, R.A 고서영 / shane.han@sks.co.kr / 3773-9992

매수(유지) 
목표주가: 640,000 원(하향) 
현재주가: 285,500 원 
상승여력: 124.2%

Signal: 3Q26, 조업일수 감소 및 환율 하락으로 시장 예상치 하회 
Key: 4Q26~1Q27 부터 본격적인 발전엔진 및 FDC 수주 모멘텀 전망 
Step: 상선 우려 감안해도 엔진&FDC 으로 밸류 매력도 충분. 매수 기회

3Q26 Review: 시장예상치 하회하는 실적 시현  
3Q26 연결 매출액 5조 7,536억원(+30.2% YoY, -9.1% QoQ), 영업이익 8,992억원
(+61.3% YoY, -13.5% QoQ)으로, 시장 예상치(9,792억원)를 하회했다. 조업일수 감소
(휴가 10 일+중대재해 4일) 및 환율 하락 영향(450 억원)과 더불어, 임단협 관련 예상 비
용 중 75%를 이번 분기 선반영 했기 때문이다. 상선 수주 빈티지는 중형 선박 기준 ‘23 년 
9.6%, ‘24년 65%, ‘25년 25%, 대형 선박 기준 ‘22년 4%, ‘23년 45%, ‘24년 31%, 
‘25년 20%로, 선가 상승효과 지속되는 중이다. 특히 해양 부문에서 루야 프로젝트의 공정
률 확대 효과 및 C/O로 180억원의 환입 발생으로 전 분기 대비 큰 폭의 이익 성장 시현했
다. 최근 키윗과 미국 내 합작 조선소 설립을 추진하며, 미 군수지원함 초도 2척 수주 및 향
후 물량 건조, 그리고 HII와의 함정 선체블록 하도급 사업 수주까지 기대할 수 있다.

투자의견 ‘매수’ 유지, 목표주가 64 만원으로 하향 
HD 현대중공업에 대한 투자의견 ‘매수’를 유지하고, 목표주가는 기존 82 만원에서 64 만원
으로 하향한다. 최근 상선 실적 피크아웃 우려에 따른 조선주 주가 급락으로 멀티플 하향 조
정했다. 하지만 상선 부문의 우려를 감안하더라도 상선 사이클과 무관한 육상 발전 엔진 및 
FDC 에 대한 글로벌 하이퍼스케일러들의 수요 확대는 지속되고 있는 중이다. 올해 공시 기
준으로 육상발전 엔진 약 1.6GW 수주한 것으로 알려졌으나, 미공시된 약 1.4GW 까지 포
함 시 총 3.0GW 이상을 수주한 상황이다. 캐파 증설 기준으로 ‘28 년 1.5GW, ‘29 년 
3GW 캐파에 해당하는 물량을 이미 대부분 채웠으며, 기존 계획 대비 빠른 준공 혹은 공정
률 확대, 더 나아가 추가적인 캐파 증설까지 고려할 수준으로 파악된다. 빠르면 올해 연말 
혹은 내년 초부터 4 행정 중속엔진&FDC 본격 수주를 통해 중장기적 신성장 동력 확보할 
전망이다. 현 주가 레벨에서 상승여력 충분히 남아있다는 판단으로, 3Q 실적 및 임단협 불
확성 해소된 현 시점에서 적극적 매수 의견 및 조선주 최선호주(Top-Pick)로 제시한다.

Document 277: AI is boosting business creations both in the US and Europe (2026-11-04)

AI IS BOOSTING BUSINESS CREATIONS BOTH IN THE US AND EUROPE
Stéphane Colliac
The significant increase in business creations since mid-2025 in the United States, France and Germany – and, to a lesser extent, in the United
Kingdom – is continuing. Across the board, this trend is being driven by the information and communication sector and mostly by AI. It is
spreading to other business services, while developments in consumer services, industry and construction are more mixed. However, in both

the United States and France, the trend is broadening, suggesting that AI is facilitating business creation across all sectors.

the colour, the larger the change. The data series are defined as follows: Germany = new entries in the companies register (start-ups) since 2008; France = business start-ups, including

micro-entrepreneurs, since 2000; United Kingdom = businesses registered for VAT/PAYE since 2017; United States = business applications (tax identification number) since 2004. Sectors

### Page 2

2
The bank for
a changing
world
Chart of the Week / 4 November 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
A noticeable boom in the United States, France, and Germany, but not quite as pronounced in the United Kingdom
The four economies for which we have comparable data (the United States, France, Germany and the United Kingdom) have all
seen, to varying degrees, an increase in the number of new business creations. While the timing varies across these countries,

most of them are enjoying strong momentum. In the United States, there has been a noticeable acceleration since the second
quarter of 2025, which has continued to strengthen. Germany has followed a similar trajectory, moving from slight declines at
the start of 2024 to sharp increases. France is experiencing the same trend but with some specific features, as momentum has
picked up significantly after months of slowdown (due to the uncertainty following the dissolution of the National Assembly) and is

accelerating across most sectors in 2026. In the United Kingdom, the trend is less pronounced and the increase may not last.
Business services, and AI in particular, are driving this momentum
In most of these four countries, business creations are rising significantly in the information and communication sector, which is
directly linked to AI. In the United States, Germany and France, there has been a pronounced trend which is gaining momentum.
In the United Kingdom, the AI sector is also seeing an increase, but it is significantly less pronounced. However, this momentum is

not limited to this sector. It is spreading to many services sectors, including finance, real estate and business services. Growth has
been sustained since mid-2025 in the United States and Germany, and since the third quarter of 2025 in France.
The picture for consumer services is more mixed. In the United States, trade, accommodation and catering have experienced major
increases in creations since mid-2025. In France, these sectors are in positive territory, particularly trade, where the increase in

business creations has recently accelerated. In Germany, these same sectors fell into negative territory in 2026 following a rather
mixed year in 2025.
The situation is even more mixed for manufacturing and construction. In the United States, these sectors are also part of the
overall positive momentum in the third quarter of 2026. This shows that AI does not only benefit sectors closely linked to it
(notably the digital technology sector) but that it makes it easier to start a business across the entire economy (by simplifying

administrative procedures in particular). In France, the upturn in business creations in the construction sector suggests that this
same trend is at play, even as activity in the sector continues to contract.
Therefore, the trend is more widespread in the United States and is becoming increasingly prevalent in France. It is driven by
specific sectors of the services industry in Germany, while it is significantly more subdued in the United Kingdom.

stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

Topics  Regulatory reform in India's insurance sector

Over the past quarter century, the Indian government has pursued the liberalization of the country's insurance market, 
with the aim of bringing stability to people's living standards and strengthening financial intermediation functions. 
Recently, however, it has begun to reverse course, tightening rules in order to better protect consumers.

■ India's insurance market has grown rapidly with deregulation 
The Indian government has long pursued 
the development of the country's insurance 
market, with the aim of bringing stability to 
people's living standards and strengthening 
financial intermediation functions. This 
stance can be attributed to two background 
factors:  
The first is that India's social security 
system remains underdeveloped. As a result, 
enrollment in health and life insurance can 
provide a vital safety net for mitigating the 
risk of sharp declines in household income 
due to unexpected illnesses or accidents.  
The second is that the sustainable 
development of the Indian economy requires 
the accumulation of long-term and stable 
domestic capital, separate from short-term 
bank deposits, in order to finance the 
development of infrastructure and the 
nurturing of industry. The hope has been that 
the enormous sums of money collected and 
managed by insurance companies can serve 
as a reliable source for this long-term 
financing.  
These factors have led the Indian 
government to walk a path of gradual 
deregulation over the past quarter century. 
The process began with the liberalization of 
market entry by private and foreign 
companies in 2000, which was followed by 
a lifting of the ban on "bancassurance" (the 
sale of insurance through bank branches) in 
2002, the raising of the ceiling on foreign 
ownership, and other reforms.  
This series of deregulatory measures 
spurred the entry of numerous private and foreign 
firms, causing market competition to intensify. Several 
Japanese insurers, including Nippon Life and the Tokio 
Marine Group, also entered the market, helping to drive 
investment in India.  
Besides deregulation related to market entry and 
sales, other factors also contributed to the rapid growth 
of India's insurance market, namely the expansion of 
the middle class, improvements in household financial 
literacy, and the digitalization of insurance services. 
Premium income grew at a pace exceeding economic 
growth, and in recent years insurance companies' assets 
under management have reached a level equivalent to 
Year Event

and Development Authority of India)

3 
incentives to sell products and inappropriate sales practices. This should shore up the credibility of the 
insurance market and the foundation for its sustainable development. 
On the minus side, there are worries about side effects arising from the transition. As rules on selling 
become stricter, insurance companies and the financial institutions acting as insurance agents will inevitably 
see some temporary moderation in the pace of their earnings growth. Moreover, given that insurance 
companies and financial institutions are major investors in India's financial markets, tighter regulation is 
likely to exert downward pressure on equity and bond prices. 
These changes in the insurance market provide an important perspective for assessing the future 
trajectory of the Indian economy. They illustrate that rapid market expansion driven by liberalization can 
create a variety of problems. And this dynamic is not limited to the insurance sector, as it can also occur 
across many other industries. At the same time, the redesign and recalibration of regulations in response to 
such developments can provide a foundation for a new stage of growth. The new insurance regulations 
could constitute an early example of this phenomenon, so will be closely watched to see what changes they 
bring in the years ahead.  
  (Shotaro Kumagai)

Topics  Talent travel curbs may weigh on China’s growth

In September, the Chinese government introduced new exit-entry rules. Indicative of an effort to retain talent in order 
to prevent technology leakage, the rules could narrow opportunities for China to absorb overseas knowledge and weigh 
on its economic growth over the medium to long term.

■ Scope of overseas travel restrictions expanded 
On September 15, the Chinese 
government brought in new rules 
specifying conditions and screening 
procedures for entry and exit. The new 
rules explicitly state that the Ministry of 
Commerce or other departments may 
prohibit Chinese citizens from leaving the 
country if they violate export-control or 
technology import/export regulations and 
are deemed likely to threaten national 
industrial or technological security.  
Under China's Exit and Entry 
Administration Law, which came into 
effect in 2013, defendants and suspects in 
criminal cases, as well as persons involved 
in unsettled civil cases whom courts have 
prohibited from leaving the country, had 
already been subject to exit restrictions. In 
addition, Chinese citizens could be denied 
permission to leave the country if the 
competent departments determined that 
their departure might endanger national 
security or interests. Aside from formal exit 
bans, personnel who handle state secrets 
and officials in the public sector have long 
been subject to controls such as centralized 
passport management and the need to 
obtain approval in advance of overseas travel. What the new rules effectively do is make it clear that exit 
can also be restricted when there are issues from the standpoint of economic security. 
Against this backdrop, it was reported at the end of September that overseas travel restrictions already 
imposed on key personnel in the AI and semiconductor sectors had been expanded to include their spouses, 
children, and other family members. According to these reports, these key personnel include prominent AI 
startup founders, senior executives at major private enterprises, and core R&D talent. Although the measure 
does not constitute a blanket travel ban, affected individuals and their families are reportedly required to 
obtain government approval for exit even in the case of short private trips or business travel. 
This regulatory tightening is thought to be related to the technological rivalry between the U.S. and China. 
As competition surrounding critical technologies such as generative AI intensifies, the Chinese government 
appears to view not only technologies themselves, but also the researchers and engineers who create them, 
as strategic assets. By widening travel curbs to encompass family members, the authorities are likely 
seeking to prevent overseas emigration by individuals or the relocation of R&D operations abroad. A 
noteworthy aspect of the recent reports is that the controls apparently now extend beyond the public sector 
to key personnel at private enterprises, as well as their families. Restrictions like these on talent mobility 
could undermine the mechanisms of technology absorption and productivity improvement that have 
hitherto facilitated China's growth.

### Page 6

ASIA MONTHLY REPORT  November 2026, No.308

The Japan Research Institute, Limited  
Economics Department

5 
■ China's productivity gains have been supported by market opening and technology 
absorption 
Productivity exerts a substantial influence on 
economic growth. Total factor productivity (TFP), an 
indicator of an economy's overall production 
efficiency, provides an estimate of efficiency in 
production that cannot be explained solely in terms of 
inputs of capital and labor, and reflects factors such as 
levels of technology and efficiency of resource 
allocation. 
China's TFP has been rising for many years. In 
addition to the transition to a market economy and the 
growth of private enterprises since the country adopted 
the policy of reform and opening up, Chinese 
productivity has benefitted from the absorption of 
foreign technology and managerial know‑how through 
trade and direct investment. A labor shift from 
agriculture to manufacturing and other sectors, along 
with competition among firms and the weeding out of 
weaker players, has also helped deliver productivity 
gains across the economy.  
Nevertheless, China's TFP remains low relative to 
other major economies. According to estimates from 
Penn World Table, China's TFP in 2023 was less than 
half that of the U.S., and it was also below the levels not 
only of Germany and other major European economies 
and of South Korea and Japan, but of Thailand and 
Malaysia, too. Factors behind this include inefficient 
resource allocation and the slow diffusion of advanced 
technologies and management practices. Preferential 
financial and institutional treatment for state‑owned 
enterprises, regional protectionism, and mechanisms 
that impede market entry and exit have distorted the 
allocation of resources, preventing capital and labor 
from being fully utilized by more productive firms and sectors. Moreover, although China has achieved 
strong competitiveness in certain advanced industries, such as electric vehicles and semiconductors, the 
leading-edge technologies and management practices developed in these sectors have not sufficiently 
diffused to the service sector and small and medium‑sized enterprises, which form the foundation of the 
economy, and this has constrained overall productivity growth.  
■ Moves to contain technology could hinder growth over the medium to long term 
Efforts to retain talent in order to prevent technology leakage could further delay China's productivity 
catch‑up with advanced economies. While restrictions on overseas travel by elite professionals may help 
with the retention of domestic R&D talent and protection of current technological advantages, they could 
also reduce opportunities to absorb overseas knowledge and to jointly develop new technologies with 
foreign partners. The transfer of technology and know‑how does not occur solely through the sharing of 
"explicit knowledge," in the form of academic papers, technical blueprints, and so on. "Tacit knowledge," 
which is transmitted through face‑to‑face discussion, collaborative work, and the acquisition of experience 
in production settings, is also crucial. If travel controls delay or prevent participation in international 
conferences, joint research projects, and technology exchanges with overseas companies, opportunities to 
absorb such knowledge may be lost, slowing technological innovation and improvements in production 
processes. Furthermore, if obtaining travel approval becomes more time‑consuming or less predictable, the 
overseas assignment or international deployment of technical personnel could also be obstructed. 
The new rules also cover the entry of foreign nationals. If uncertainty surrounding the administration of 
entry and exit controls increases, it could create obstacles to attracting and retaining highly skilled talent 
from abroad. At a time when inefficient resource allocation and the slow diffusion of advanced knowledge 
are already weighing on productivity growth in China, curbs on people-to- people exchange could further

impede both the absorption and creation of knowledge, as well as its practical application. Although the 
magnitude of the impact will depend on the scope of application and actual implementation on the ground, 
restrictions on talent exchanges and overseas travel that reflect excessive emphasis on technology 
protection could slow China's TFP growth over the medium to long term and act as a headwind to the 
Chinese economy by lowering its potential growth rate.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
As expected, the RBA Monetary Policy Board (MPB) lifted
the cash rate 25bps to 4.85% at its November meeting.
This follows the increase at the previous (late-September)
meeting and is a marked turnaround from the outlook back
in August. The post-meeting media statement cited higher

global energy prices resulting from the Middle East conflict,
tighter domestic capacity pressures and an increase in
inflation expectations for the decision.
However, the decision was a split vote, 6 votes to 3, a
possibility we have flagged for some time. In the post-meeting
media conference, Governor Bullock explained that the
dissenting minority put more weight on the downside risks and
did not see signs of wages growth picking up as much as the

staff forecasts implied. Those members were reportedly more
confident that slower growth would prevent higher inflation
expectations becoming entrenched.
Financial conditions are restrictive and this is expected to
bring inflation down over time. Because the RBA assesses the
risks to the inflation outlook to still be skewed to the upside,
the outlook for coming meetings will still be for a decision
between holding and hiking. But we note that the post-meeting

statement omitted September’s language “including increasing
the cash rate target further if needed” from the commitment to
do what is necessary to get inflation down.
In the post-meeting media conference, the Governor
commented that after five hikes, this is probably a good time
to “take stock”. Together with the dissents, we read this as
confirming our current view that the MPB will keep rates
on hold in December. There is also considerable data flow

between now and then, which could go either way, including
the Q3 national accounts.
Similarly, a lot could happen between now and February, but
we see it as the next most likely timing for a hike, should one
eventuate. We still see that as a risk rather than a base case.
Revised forecasts published in the accompanying Statement
on Monetary Policy (SMP) show underlying inflation lingering
around current rates for longer. This contrasts with the
previous SMP forecasts in August, which showed trimmed

mean inflation returning to the 2–3%yr target range by late
2027 and a little below the 2.5%yr target midpoint in 2028.
We note, however, that the conditioning assumptions on oil
prices, which are based on futures prices, were at a local peak
at the time the forecasts were finalised.
RBA hikes cash rate to new high of 4.85%, a December pause likely
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
The RBA’s GDP growth forecasts for 2027 and 2028 were
both revised down –0.3%pts to 1.3% for 2027 and 1.5% in

2028, despite an upward revision to the near term. This is
well below even the RBA’s downbeat view of potential output
growth. Within the GDP aggregate, recent misses and leading
indicators have required upward revisions to the near-term
outlooks for business and dwelling investment, and downside
revisions to public demand. These revisions were offset further
out with downward revisions to the 2028 outlook for business
and dwelling investment and an upward revision to public

demand. Household consumption forecasts were revised down
across most of the profile.
With the unemployment rate already at 4.7% in August and
September – a rate the RBA did not forecast until end-2027 in
its August SMP forecasts – the RBA has lifted its unemployment
forecast to show a peak of 5.1% in at end-2028, the end of the
forecast horizon. This largely reflects stronger labour supply,
with forecasts for employment growth essentially unchanged.

We have repeatedly flagged that cost-of-living pressures are
likely to keep people in the labour force and thus boost the
participation rate. The RBA had previously not factored this
adequately into its forecasts but acknowledged in the SMP
that this mechanism had kept participation higher than it had
expected at the time of the August SMP.
However, because inflation surprised to the upside recently,
this additional labour supply has had no bearing on the

RBA’s assessment of capacity pressures. The RBA assesses
the domestic economy to still be a little tight, and tighter
than previously judged. The RBA’s framework for assessing
the degree of spare capacity or tightness in the economy
necessarily translates an upside surprise on inflation (or unit
labour costs) into a conclusion that the economy must be
tighter than previously judged. Indeed, the RBA’s estimate
of the NAIRU (unemployment rate consistent with inflation

stable at target) has been revised up to 4.75%.
The RBA highlighted that elevated inflation expectations may
be contributing to higher realised inflation. Consumer inflation
expectations have started to lift again but remain below where
they peaked earlier this year. However, the RBA appears
“RBA lifts cash rate 25bps to 4.85% in split
vote, citing “broadly based” domestic inflation
pressures, Middle East conflict and AI boom."

to be putting more attention on the inflation expectations
of firms and the frequency of mentions of pricing in their
liaison program. This is putting a lot of weight on proprietary
information that is not clearly validated by other, longer-
running data sources.

The AI boom is heavily cited in the SMP and other post-
meeting communication. The RBA has lifted its forecasts
for major trading partners, especially high-income Asian
economies that are the centre of high-tech manufacturing.
Demand for AI-related goods is also cited as a driver of both
global and domestic inflation. And although the RBA has
revised up its near-term forecasts for business investment
on the back of strong data centre activity, it is working on

the assumption that Australia does not become ‘a significant
regional hub for data centres’. Nor does it allow for any boost
to productivity trends from the technology, even in 2028. This
is a very different view of the risks from AI than the RBNZ
expressed last week. For the RBA, it is all about inflation, a
little bit about pressure on domestic construction resources,
and nothing about either an ugly unwind of the current
financing boom or a future productivity dividend.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: whiplash
total to $11.1bn. This is another clear marker of the scale of
Australia’s data-centre investment boom, which should feature
prominently in both business investment and imports in the Q3
National Accounts.
Offshore, the world has been focused on the US mid-term

elections. In the House, Senate and a series of State Governor
races, the Democrats experienced resounding victory. Come
2027, the Democrats will have a comfortable majority in both
houses of Congress and are expected to make use of this
strength to respond to voter concerns, particularly over cost-
of-living pressures. The election result also limits President
Trump’s ability to fund an extended military action against
Iran. Notably, both immediately before and after the election,

President Trump and the administration have exhibited a greater
interest in furthering negotiations with Iran. It is therefore not
a surprise that Brent oil has backed off its most recent highs
around USD118 last Friday, although the scale of the drop to
near USD93 yesterday is arguably too optimistic. Brent has since
traded back up to above USD99, still 16% below Friday’s peak.
While the initial reaction to the US election result was a
robust broad-based rally in major equity indexes, concerns

over the potential for tighter regulation of AI and the related
infrastructure buildout jolted US equities lower overnight – the
Nasdaq falling 5.5% and S&P500 4.4%, both a multiple of
the prior session’s gains. That said, US term interest rates and
FOMC rate expectations have also been scaled back; so, while
tech may come under greater scrutiny, a foundation for better
outcomes across the rest of the US economy may become
evident in time. US data out this week continued to suggest

their economy is in robust health overall. And, if tensions in
the Middle East abate, the FOMC’s concerns over US inflation
should subside. Gaining enough comfort for the FOMC to not
hike in December is a high bar, but the probability of further
hikes in 2027 should continue to fall.
Turning then to the UK, the Bank of England raised Bank Rate
by 25bps to 4.00% at their November meeting as expected.
On the Bank's baseline expectation, UK inflation is forecast to

rise from 3.5%yr at September to 5.00% by mid-2027. Activity
data has also surprised to the upside in recent months, though
"there remains limited evidence of knock-on effects on other
prices and wages".
In Australia, the RBA delivered another 25bp rate increase
at its November meeting, taking the cash rate to 4.85%. The
move was widely expected, reflecting the Board’s continued
concern around inflation following the recent run higher in
global energy prices, firmer domestic capacity pressures and

signs that inflation expectations have edged up. Inflation
is now not expected to return to the mid-point of the
target band until mid-2028. This is despite weaker growth
expectations and the unemployment rate trending up to
5.1% by end-2028 on the RBA’s forecasts.
Importantly, though, the decision was not unanimous, with
three members preferring to remain on hold given downside
risks to activity and the restraining influence of slower growth.
Governor Bullock’s suggestion that it was time to “take stock”

after five rate increases also struck a less hawkish note,
while the statement dropped September’s explicit reference
to raising rates further “if needed”. In our view, February
represents the next plausible opportunity for another hike, but
we see this only as a risk.
Policy’s effect on housing is becoming increasingly visible.
Cotality home prices fell a further 1.2% in October to now
be 6.7% below March’s peak. The cumulative price decline
is approaching the scale of previous housing corrections but

appears to have regained momentum after September’s rate
increase. Dwelling approvals saw a 5.1% headline gain in
September, but this was driven by volatile unit approvals while
detached house approvals fell sharply, suggesting higher rates
and the established-market downturn are beginning to weigh
on the construction pipeline.
Official data on nominal household spending meanwhile
rose 0.6% in September and 2.1% for Q3; however, much of
the recent acceleration looks to be price-driven, particularly

for fuel. Real spending volumes expanded by a much more
modest (but still solid) 0.7% in Q3.
The Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel provides a granular look at
income, spending and saving flows. The latest update suggests
that, beneath the resilient headline story, households are
increasingly being squeezed, particularly those with low savings
buffers. With another rate increase now delivered, housing
wealth declining and sentiment weak, Q3 increasingly looks like

the high-water mark for growth in consumer spending.
Lastly for Australia, in September the goods trade surplus
widened sharply to $2.8bn, with non-monetary gold trade
yet again adding considerable noise month-to-month. The
most notable development was another exceptionally strong
result for data-processing equipment imports, taking the Q3
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
No deals are done
This week’s developments underscore our message that
a rate hike at the RBNZ’s December meeting is far from a
done deal. New Zealand’s labour market remains softer
than expected, and that will be noted by those on the

policy committee who question the extent of second-round
inflation pressures. The rout in the US mid-term elections
has also taken significant heat out of oil prices for now,
although it’s unclear whether that situation will hold through
to next month. Our view on the endpoint for this OCR cycle
remains unchanged, but there’s a growing risk that the RBNZ
could slow the pace of tightening in the new year.
The September quarter labour market surveys pointed to

ongoing softness in the jobs market. The unemployment
rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7%, continuing a four-year run
of increases, and reaching its highest level since 2015.
Employment was softer than expected, and wage growth
remains subdued despite increasing cost-of-living pressures.
The Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS) showed a surprise
0.1% fall in employment in the September quarter, after a
downwardly revised 0.3% increase in the June quarter. That

went against the Monthly Employment Indicator (MEI) measure
of filled jobs, which had suggested some upward momentum
emerging in the jobs market (and notably, the HLFS measure
had outpaced the MEI for the previous nine straight quarters).
The fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a fall in
the labour force participation rate from 70.6% to 70.4%.
These co-movements essentially reversed the surprisingly
large increases that we saw in the June quarter, which we had

suggested at the time were more likely due to sampling error
in the survey rather than a genuine surge.
We generally recommend focusing on the unemployment rate,
which is less vulnerable to sampling errors – and the message
here is one of softness. Our best interpretation is that, to the
extent that jobs are growing, it’s not fast enough to absorb the
growth in the population (which itself is a fairly low bar, as net
migration remains at a low ebb).

Even with the economy returning to growth over the last
year or so, hiring activity has remained weak. This may be
an ongoing legacy of employment trends in previous years.
Businesses have told us that they felt burned by the severe
skill shortages that developed over 2021-23, and looked to
hold on to workers to the extent they could during the 2024-
25 downturn. The flipside of this is that they may now find
themselves overstaffed even as activity picks up again.

This shows up most strikingly in labour force transition rates.
The rise in unemployment over the last few years has not
been driven by an increase in job losses, but by accumulation
of people who, once out of work, are finding it increasingly
difficult to get back in again. That also extends to young
people trying to enter the workforce for the first time – youth
unemployment of around 25% is the highest in the history of
the survey going back to 1986.

The Labour Cost Index (LCI) rose by 0.5% in the September
quarter, in line with our expectations overall but a bit weaker
in the details – private sector wages were up by 0.5%, while
there was a surprisingly large 0.7% increase in public sector
wages. There was a notable jump in the number of employers
citing cost-of-living adjustments as a reason for increasing pay
rates – although the size of those increases varied a great deal,
with many substantially less than the headline inflation rate.

Potential RBNZ implications
The labour market data was unequivocally weaker than
expected by either us or the RBNZ. While likely not a
gamechanger for the cycle overall, it will add ammunition to the
arguments of the doves on the Monetary Policy Committee that
a pause in interest rate rises will make sense soon – perhaps in
December or early next year. It’s likely the case that interest rate
markets were over-pricing the odds of a hike at the 9 December

MPS, so the subsequent pullback in pricing makes sense.
The message from the RBNZ in the October meeting was to
be careful about pricing in "done deals", and that the outlook
Michael Gordon
Senior Economist

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
will be data-dependent. As we noted at the time, alongside
measures of inflation expectations, the key domestic data
to watch ahead of the RBNZ’s 9 December meeting will
be indicators of what is happening to excess capacity (the
output gap), as this will be a key determinant of the Bank’s

assessment of the outlook for inflation over the coming year.
The RBNZ looks at a broad suite of indicators of labour market
slack, although their research found that only four of them
seem to have predictive power: the unemployment rate,
the vacancy to unemployment ratio, the QSBO measure of
labour as the main limiting factor to growth, and the rate of
transition between jobs. The first three of these all pointed
to a softening in the September quarter. (The fourth one is

calculated from income tax data, so it’s not publicly available
and even the RBNZ receives it only with a lag.)
In the eyes of the dovish MPC members, the softness in
the labour market indicators will likely push in the direction
of having greater comfort with the medium-term inflation
outlook. The outcome of the election, with the possibility of a
return to a dual inflation/employment mandate, could increase
the prominence of these factors. This likely won’t be an explicit

factor in the RBNZ’s reasoning at its December review, but it
may be in the minds of individual MPC members.
That said, it’s important to remember that in the RBNZ’s
forecasting framework, the GDP figures are the main
determinant of excess capacity in the economy. The most
recent GDP data tells us that the output gap probably
narrowed a bit through the June quarter, mainly reflecting
historical revisions. In light of the latest labour market figures,

there may be some downside risk to the outlook for Q3 GDP –
but there are conflicting indicators there, so it’s not that clear.
At last month’s surprise on-hold OCR decision, Governor
Breman lamented that there was no protocol for the RBNZ
to be able to signal how it was reading the economic data –
in particular the softer than expected Q3 CPI print – ahead
of the policy meeting. In that light, it will be interesting to
see whether they use some already-scheduled speaking

engagements to signal what they make of the labour market
data. Top of mind is Chief Economist Conway's 10 November
speech to the UBS Australasia Conference (pointedly titled
“How is the NZ economy tracking”). Governor Breman is also
speaking to the Institute of Directors on 19 November.
Our view remains that the December OCR hike is more likely
than not. But there will be a clear debate to be had between
the MPC hawks and doves, and it may again come down to a

vote. We still expect a 4% peak in the OCR for this cycle, but
the time taken to get there could be more flexible. Takes along
the lines of "3% is the terminal rate" seem rather hasty on the
basis of a single data release.
Aside from how the Committee interprets the labour market
data, the key considerations in December will be: the RBNZ’s
surveys of inflation expectations by households and businesses;
whether the US mid-term elections lead to a sustained de-

escalation in the Middle East and a pullback in oil prices; and
whether developments in the AI space exacerbate some MPC
members’ concerns about the global economic environment.
And then of course there’s the result of the General Election,
with voting closing on Saturday evening. The actual formation
of a government is likely to take weeks of negotiation, so the
outcome may not be known by the 9 December MPS but at least
the range of possibilities should be clearer. We’ll publish our

initial thoughts on the election results early on Monday morning.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Oct Retail Card Spending (%mth)
Nov 12, Last: –0.4, Westpac f/c: 1.4
We’re forecasting a 1.4% rise in retail spending in October.
While that would be a solid increase, it’s in large part due to
the 11% rise in petrol prices over the past month. Stripping out

fuel spending, we’re forecasting a 0.6% rise in core spending
categories. Core spending has been very choppy recently,
and the increase we’re assuming follows falls in the past few
months. A result in line with our expectations would leave the
trend in core spending relatively flat since the start of this year
with the rise in fuel prices weighing on spending in other areas.
AUS: Nov Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment (index)
Nov 10, Last: 84.4
Consumer sentiment fell 4.7% to 80.4 in Oct, returning to the

deeply pessimistic levels seen earlier in the year. Responses
over the course of the survey week showed a very sharp
deterioration after the RBA rate hike.
The Nov survey is in the field over the week ended Nov 7.
Interest rates will again factor with the RBA raising the cash
rate by another 25bps to 4.85%, an 18yr high. Average pump
prices also pushed higher, the national average just under
$2.40/litre, while the housing market downturn has continued

to broaden and deepen. The US mid-term election results may
have some effects on sentiment locally as well.
AUS: Q3 Housing Finance Approvals (%qtr)
Nov 11 Last: –5.2, Westpac f/c: –10
Market f/c: –8.5, Range: –15 to –5
The total value of housing finance approvals fell The value of
new dwelling finance approvals excl. refinance fell 5.2%qtr,
the largest quarterly drop in three years. Annual growth eased
to 7.3%yr. The weakness was accounted for mainly by lower

lending volumes: loan numbers were down 4.0%qtr (–0.6%yr),
while average loan size also contributed negatively.
Q3 is expected to see an even bigger decline with the full
impact of interest rate rises and tax policy changes showing
through. The value of both loan enquiries and turnover fell by
around 10% in the quarter. We expect total finance approvals
to show a 10% drop as well, with investor loan approvals likely
to be down 16%qtr and owner occupier approvals down 6%qtr.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
US: Oct CPI (%mth)
Nov 11, Headline, Last: 0.6, Market f/c: 0.3, Westpac f/c: 0.4
US consumer prices rose by 0.6% in September, although
half of the gain was due to energy inflation, with core prices
instead up 0.3%. Over the year, prices were reported to have

risen 3.7%yr overall and 2.6%yr excluding food and energy.
Constructively, ex food, energy and shelter, annual inflation
met the FOMC’s medium-term target of 2.0%yr.
Come October, energy price pressures are set to recede
given the pre-election run higher in oil prices came late in the
month. A softer print for shelter is also likely to moderate core
momentum. We see a 0.4% gain for the headline measure and
a 0.2% result for core inflation as most probable. Looking to

2027, disinflation is expected to continue at a measured pace
towards the medium-term target.

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 26, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Oil Price Dynamics Remain Key to the Outlook
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
Inflation, interest rates and equity market dynamics have changed materially since mid-
August, as widening attacks on Middle East and Russian crude oil and refining capacity
worsened supply and price conditions. Indeed, with the oil market already strained, an
early-September attack on a major Saudi Arabian export pipeline sharply raised concerns

about a protracted and deepening oil crisis. While Middle East crude exports eventually
managed to significantly rebound, increased security risks and freight costs raised
physical crude prices.
Against this backdrop, the spot Brent price surged from $92/barrel in mid-August
back into a $115 to $135/barrel price range until recently. Reflecting unusual market
tightness, other North Sea oil grades were recently trading even higher, up to $160/
barrel, according to the International Energy Agency (IEA). Even as it cooled from $135

to $115/barrel between October 2 and October 20, spot Brent is still almost double
year-ago levels, and so are many diesel prices. The AAA average U.S. retail diesel price
recently hit a new record and was 74% higher year-over-year (YoY) as of October 20.
With insufficient regional supply, the New York Harbor wholesale diesel price is 130%
higher from a year ago, according to the Energy Information Administration (EIA).
Indeed, sharply higher oil prices and a growing diesel shortage have put the global diesel

market under extreme strain. The U.S., for example, has little distillate cushion and
limited spare refining capacity. The distillate inventory (diesel and fuel oil) has decreased
from 37 days of demand coverage in January to just 26 days by early October, according
to the EIA. This 30% drop brought the supply buffer to the bottom of its historical
range. With limited refining capacity and reliance on imports for about 10% of regional
consumption, East Coast distillate inventories are the lowest since 1990, for example,

and are expected to remain below their 5-year average into late 2027, according to EIA
data. More broadly, according to the IEA’s October Oil Market Report (OMR), “oil market
balances are forecast to remain in deficit through the end of the year, with constrained
middle distillate supplies likely to persist well into 2027.”
Thus, although wage growth/labor demand do not appear inflationary, the oil crisis
risks continuing to feed “core” inflation. According to the Federal Reserve’s Beige Book1

covering precisely the mid-August to early-October period of heightened oil-market
turmoil, “higher oil prices permeated all District reports,” with firms noting that fuel
surcharges were passing on increased costs throughout the supply chain. The 22% rise
in airfares over the past year alone contributed around 0.2 percentage point (pp) to the
2.6% 12-month increase in the “core” consumer price index (CPI) for September. Aside
from transportation costs effects, the spike in diesel prices also portends high food

prices with a few-month lag given planting/harvesting cost considerations.
By solidifying expectations for higher Fed interest rates to fight inflation, the surge in oil
prices has contributed to the spike in long-term Treasury yields this year. According to an
October 13, 2026, Empirical Research Partners report, the Iran-war oil crisis is estimated
to have contributed about 0.50 pp to the roughly 2.40 pp increase in the real U.S. long-
term Treasury rate from 2020 to early October (Exhibit 1A). According to the report, this

oil-price effect accounts for “much of the rise in real rates since February 27, 2026...”
On the positive side, while most other underpinnings for higher rates compared to the
pre-pandemic decade are structural (Exhibit 1A), the oil-price-related effect on interest
rates is reversible, implying that a resolution to the current oil crisis has the potential to
help lower both inflation and interest rates, relieving some of the current headwinds to
growth and asset prices.

Indeed, while much of the increase in interest rates since the pandemic reflects stronger,
more normal economic growth, and has thus been accompanied by surging corporate
1 Summary of Commentary on Current Economic Conditions by Federal Reserve District, Federal Reserve, October 2026.
Investment Implications
Oil market conditions remain a swing
factor for the outlook. A shift lower in
oil risk premia and improvement on
the refining front would go a long way

toward easing inflation and interest
rates while bolstering risk-asset
appetite. A gradual increase in Fixed
Income portfolio duration continues to
make sense, in our view.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 26, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
earnings and risk-asset prices, worsening oil-market conditions since mid-summer and
associated spike in yields have been accompanied by a loss of momentum in U.S. equity
prices (Exhibit 1B). Given their higher sensitivity to energy price and interest rates shocks,
Small-caps lost relatively more momentum than Large-caps during this period.
The Large-cap rally also faded, though, as oil and interest rate headwinds widened stock-

level return dispersion, with roughly 40% of constituent stocks in bear market territory
(down 20% or more). Still, buoyed by the Technology sector as well as by expectations
of conflict resolution, the market-capitalization weighted S&P 500 Index has mainly
consolidated just below its peak. While the equal-weighted S&P 500 Index stumbled more,
it was just about 3% off its peak as of October 20.
While the economy remains resilient and profit expectations remain strong, persistent

oil market turmoil makes the macro backdrop more fragile. For example, the Beige Book
not only highlights concern about spreading fuel-related inflation, as noted above, but its
language also suggests that economic activity lost some momentum since its mid-July
to late-August report. Overall, it describes growth as slight to moderate, with generally
moderate manufacturing growth except for robust activity in the energy-heavy Dallas
District. Little growth is reported in other sectors overall, with most Districts seeing little

to no change in consumer spending, for example.
Employment was described as stable to little changed, with generally adequate labor supply
except for certain specialized trade workers. Five of twelve Districts note some reports of
businesses cutting staff through attrition, delaying hiring or anticipating less job creation
as a result of AI-driven productivity gains. Most Districts reported that business maintained
a positive outlook for coming months, though “expectations tend to be for slight growth,

weighted toward manufacturing and burdened by persistent uncertainty.”
The September employment and retail sales reports seem to validate the anecdotal Beige
Book evidence. Also, the 0.4% annualized manufacturing production growth over the July to
September period (and 1.1% YoY, according to the Federal Reserve Broad), while welcome,
is hardly an indication of booming conditions. Against a backdrop of rising electricity prices,
wage growth lagging inflation, and higher gasoline prices broadly offsetting this year’s tax

cut benefits, real consumer spending is unlikely to accelerate much absent stronger labor
demand and real wage growth. A resolution to the oil crisis that brings economic relief and
tempers interest rates is thus critical for continued expansion in 2027, in our view.

Investment Implications
Portfolio maintenance is a key
component of successful investing.
It’s time for investors to assess their
portfolios—or look under the hood.
MARKET VIEW
Six Questions Investors Should Be Asking Themselves Right Now
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
After four years of rising equity prices, investors have plenty to celebrate. Despite

inflation scares, geopolitical tensions, trade wars, rising interest rates and breathless
predictions around AI, the S&P 500 has returned some 131% (total returns) since the
bull market in equities commenced in October 2022. Corporate earnings have generally
exceeded expectations; AI has ignited a historic investment boom, and America’s
technology giants have powered the market to new heights.
We believe the bull will rumble on into 2027. We also believe that now is the time for

investors to take stock of their portfolios, since bull markets have a way of changing the
composition of portfolios. In addition, investors should not assume that the investment
strategies that worked well in the past four years will necessarily deliver comparable
results during the next four. Below are six questions investors should be asking
themselves right now:
Number One: How concentrated is my portfolio? Many investors believe they are
invested in the stock market when, in fact, they are invested in a handful of companies—

specifically AI-related names that have soared and dominated the index over the past
few years. Information technology now accounts for roughly 40% of the market cap of
the S&P 500, while the top 10 companies represent a share of 38.4%.
As the Financial Times recently noted, U.S. equity and credit markets have become so
increasingly concentrated in AI-related groups that “more than two-thirds of companies
in the Russell 1000 index of large U.S. groups are linked to AI, either directly or

indirectly or because the technology is becoming embedded in their business models.”2
Meanwhile, Big Technology hyperscalers and companies that are beneficiaries of the AI
spending boom make up about 16% of the U.S. high-grade bond market after a huge
borrowing binge, making the grouping the largest in the investment-grade bond market.2
The upshot: Investors that have been rewarded by concentration need to ask themselves
what percentage of their wealth is tied to the all-encompassing AI trade and consider

what would happen if mega-cap tech leadership falters.
Number Two: How diversified is my portfolio? While concentration asks whether
investors own too much of a few assets, diversification asks whether investors own enough
of different assets that behave differently when economic cycles or financial conditions
change. A portfolio containing hundreds of U.S. Equities via exchange-traded funds, mutual
funds and other assets may appear diversified. But if those companies are overwhelmingly

exposed to the same economic cycle, interest rate environment, or technology theme,
the diversification benefits are diluted. Case in point: While we remain constructive on
Emerging Market (EM) Equities, investors need to understand that a significant driver of
EM returns has come from AI-driven markets like Taiwan and South Korea.
Today, true diversification means considering exposure to hard assets (commodities, real
estate), EM mining and agricultural leaders, EM bonds and currencies, China bio-technology,

Small-cap international, and Private Equity and Hedge Funds for qualified investors.
Number Three: How tax-efficient is my portfolio? Bull markets create wealth—in
addition to tax liabilities. The good news is that after four years of rising equity returns,
investors are sitting on sizable, unrealized capital gains. The bad news: Market gains
can feel like a financial straitjacket, making it difficult to rebalance portfolios without
triggering significant tax liabilities.

Against this backdrop, now is the time for investors to review where the assets are
held—distinguishing between taxable and tax-advantaged accounts, and the attendant tax
ramifications. The appropriate mix depends on individual circumstances, including future
2 “Investors seek shelter from heightened exposure to AI”, The Financial Times, October 6, 2026.

### Page 5

5 of 8 October 26, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
withdrawal taxes. It is also time to consider tax-loss strategies, charitable donations of
securities and other tactics and strategies since in the end, the measure of investment
success isn’t simply how much money investors make. It’s how much they keep.
Number Four: Is it time to take profits?* There is a peculiarity to bull markets: The
more money investors make, the more the attendant tax liabilities. Tax liabilities, in turn,

can discourage selling, and the reluctance to sell can leave investors dangerously exposed
to yesterday’s leaders and compound concentration risks. That brings up an emotionally
difficult decision: When should investors sell their leaders or take some chips off the table?
The question is notably relevant today, given four years of substantial Equity gains.
Beyond tax considerations, investors need to understand that profit-taking can create
financial flexibility. Realized after-tax gains can replenish cash reserves, fund near-

term spending needs, reduce leverage or most importantly, provide capital to rebalance
portfolios in other areas of the market. In general, the goal of taking profits isn’t to get
out of the market—it is to ensure that after years of accumulated gains, these proceeds
are redeployed and redistributed across various asset classes that continue to support
and underpin long-term wealth accumulation.
Number Five: How well is my portfolio positioned for a new interest rate

regime? For much of the past quarter century, investors operated in a world of copious
liquidity, declining interest rates and relatively cheap capital. The era of capital abundance
favored stocks over bonds, but that era is over—we are shifting to a world of capital
scarcity, or intense competition for capital and the attendant hike in the cost of capital.
In this world, higher rates weigh the most on long-duration assets. Positioning for this
regime favors companies with strong balance sheets and cash flow over firms with heavy

debt or refinancing needs. High-quality bonds are more attractive in this environment;
sovereign debt risks are concentrated more overseas (i.e., France) than the U.S.
Number Six: How well is my portfolio positioned for the next shock? Shocks
happen. If the past four years have taught investors anything, it is that the unexpected
should be the expected. This decade alone has produced gut-wrenching market moves
related to a global pandemic, a ground war in Europe, the reconfiguration of global

supply chains, epic technological change and a war in the Middle East. Through it all, U.S.
Equities have trended higher but hardly in a linear fashion.
The next shocks could emerge in the global bond market, where mounting government
debt and higher borrowing costs have triggered mounting volatility. It could emerge from
disappointing returns on massive AI investments. Or it could come from a geopolitical
event, another energy shock or any other unforeseen event. Investors can’t predict the

next shock, but they can prepare for it. So now is the time for investors to stress test
their portfolios against a potential shock, examining how a steep downdraft in Equities
or spike in bond yields would affect their retirement plans, liquidity needs and other
financial goals and objectives.
Exhibit 2: Six Questions to Stress Test Your Portfolio.
The six questions What investors should examine Portfolio diagnostic
1. How concentrated am I? Mega-cap technology exposure and overlapping holdings % of portfolio in top 10 stocks

2. Am I truly diversified? Asset allocation, geographic mix and correlated exposures Current equity/bond mix versus target
3. How tax-efficient am I? Unrealized gains, asset location and tax-loss harvesting Embedded capital gains and estimated tax cost of rebalancing
4. Is it time to take profits? Portfolio drift, oversized winners and valuations Positions exceeding target allocation
5. Am I ready for higher interest rates? Duration, leverage, refinancing risks and cash flows Bond duration and exposure to highly leveraged companies

6. Am I prepared for the next shock? Liquidity, downside exposure and portfolio resilience Estimated loss in a 20% equity selloff
Source: Chief Investment Office. Data as of October 22, 2026.
* “Pop the Champagne,” Portfolio Insights, Chief Investment Office, August 2026.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 26, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What are rising credit spreads signaling about the broader market environment?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Officer
Rising French bond yields with a sharp rise in spreads above German bunds, mixed
with AI buildout concerns, are creating nervousness across the capital markets. This
nervousness centers around the ability to fund deficits, the potential for slower growth

and the future return on the announced capital investment. We expect these concerns
to continue to linger well into next year as growth worries remain in France, bond
yields stay sticky around current levels, and, in the case of AI, investors demand more
compensation for financing.
At this stage, we do not expect the rising spreads in targeted areas to be a precursor
for a material slowdown in economic growth overall. Rather, we believe this is a natural

signal that investors are moving up the quality stack as we move into the middle of
the AI buildout. The bottom line is that rising credit spreads in lower quality corners of
the market are natural along the lifecycle of financing. When free cash flow is called
into question, or when debt financing costs begin to approach the expected return on
investment, investors begin to reprice risk.
We continue to emphasize higher quality balance sheets in corporate and sovereign

credit as well as higher quality leaders in the AI buildout overall. The closer we get to
2027, investors will likely begin to transition from too much exposure to the “buildout”
and not enough exposure to “AI adopters.”
